A.2.
DBQuery

The DBQuery benchmark models a database query-plan tree, a common
representation in cost-based optimizers and execution
planners
(
22
)
. Following the standard iterator
execution model
(
10
)
, internal nodes represent
operations such as joins and filters, while leaf nodes represent
data access operations like sequential or index scans.
This tree-based topological structure is heavily utilized in modern
database benchmarking and performance prediction
workloads
(
29
)
.

The suite implements a variety of plan-analysis and mutation passes
common in query planning. These include bottom-up aggregations for cost
and cardinality (
sumCost
,
sumRows
), topological
analysis (
countJoins
), memory-pressure estimations
(
sumMemory
,
hashJoinPressure
), and map-style
metadata updates (
scaleCosts
,
clearQueryFlags
).

Results are reported in

Table
19
.

DecisionTree

Decision Trees are omnipresent in classification algorithms
(
4
)
.
The
DecisionTree
benchmark models a binary classification tree used in
inference and model-inspection pipelines. Leaves store label/sample metadata,
and internal nodes store metadata such as threshold, feature, and child subtrees.

The workload is fold-dominated and includes structural traversals
(
countNodes
,
countLeaves
,
treeDepth
),
statistics and diagnostics (
sumImpurity
,
sumSamples
,

countFeatureUses
,
countSmallLeaves
,

sumPathLengths
,
inferenceCost
), and inference queries
(
classify
,
classifyDepth
,

). Results are reported in

A.5.
LinearListReduction

This benchmark uses a wide linear list node with multiple scalar payload
fields, representing workloads where each record carries more fields than a
single pass consumes.

The benchmark pass (
reduction
, implemented by
reduce
)
folds over the list while consuming only one field. This stresses dead-field
handling: flattened still steps over all inlined fields, while factored can skip work on
unused buffers. Results are reported in
Table
22
.
The dead field properly is more useful in practive for fold-like benchmarks as
opposed to map-like traversals. Since functional programs always create new values,
map like traversals still need to read and write dead fields to new regions.

Table 22
.

LinearListReduction
. ADT fields: 11; factored buffers: 11. Times in ms.

A.6.
ReduceNestedList

This benchmark models a nested list where each outer node carries a scalar
payload and an embedded inner list. The reduction pass consumes the scalar
payload while skipping over the nested list structure, making it a compact test
of dead-data elimination in a recursive setting.

A.10.
OctTree

OctTrees are the data structure of choice when it comes to
problems that require spatial partioning. For instance,
N-body simulation – physics-related simulation such as computing forces under a
system of particles etc. They are also widely used in ray tracing,
problems as Bounding Volume Hierarchies.
(
2
,
16
)

and other image proccessing applications.
Our
OctTree
benchmark models a physics simulation related octree using
constructors
Cell
,
Particle
, and
EmptyOct
. This
representation is inspired from hierarchical N-body and multipole-style
computation.

The workload includes fold-like passes
(
sumMass
,
sumEnergy
,
countActive
,

Appendix B
Additional Compiler Material

B.1.
Turning datatype descriptions into location structures

Algorithm 1

Generating an factored location from an ADT

1:

Input:
datatype:
\tau
, environment:
\Delta

2:

Output:
factored location variable
loc_{\mathsf{soa}}={l_{d}}^{r_{d}}\,\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{(K,i,loc_{i})}}{\overarrow@\to{(K,i,loc_{i})}}{\overarrow@\to{(K,i,loc_{i})}}{\overarrow@\to{(K,i,loc_{i})}}

3:

Notation:

K
: Data Constructor,
i
: Field Index,
\sigma
: Field Type.

4:

function

BuildSoALoc
(
\tau,\Delta
)

5:

ctors\leftarrow\textsc{ConstructorsOf}(\tau,\Delta)

\triangleright

[(K,[(i,\sigma)])]

6:

flds\leftarrow[\,]

7:

for all

(K,fields)\in ctors

do

8:

xs\leftarrow\textsc{Entries}(\tau,K,fields,\Delta)

9:

flds\leftarrow\textsc{Append}(flds,xs)

10:

end

for

11:

l_{d}^{r_{d}}\leftarrow\textsc{FreshSingleLoc}

12:

return

introLocVec
(
l_{d}^{r_{d}},flds
)

13:

end

function

14:

function

Entries
(
\tau,K,fields,\Delta
)

15:

entries\leftarrow[\,]

16:

for all

(i,\sigma)\in fields

do

17:

if

\sigma\neq\tau

then

\triangleright
skip self-recursive fields

18:

if

\textsc{IsPackedTy}(\sigma)

then

19:

loc_{i}\leftarrow\textsc{BuildSoALoc}(\sigma,\Delta)

20:

else

21:

loc_{i}\leftarrow\textsc{FreshSingleLoc}

22:

end

if

23:

entries\leftarrow\textsc{Append}(entries,\ [(K,i,loc_{i})])

24:

end

if

25:

end

for

26:

return

entries

27:

end

function

Algorithm
1
shows how algebraic datatypes turn into factored locations formally.
Given a datatype
\tau
and environment
\Delta
,
ConstructorsOf
returns constructors and their fields as
(K,(i,\sigma))
pairs.
For each non-self-recursive field, the algorithm emits an entry
(K,i,loc_{i})
: scalar fields allocate a fresh single-buffer location, while packed fields recursively build a nested factored location.
After collecting these entries, the algorithm allocates one disjoint data-constructor buffer location
{l_{d}}^{r_{d}}
and introduces the final location variable with
introLocVec
.

This pseudocode presents the factored case, where fields which are non self recursive data type definitions are also factored.
In the
Colobus
compiler, HiCal annotations can additionally choose flattened or factored for packed subfields within a datatype selected for factored layout, we call this a hybrid factored layout.

B.2.
Example of working with mutable cursors in NoCal

⬇

sumTree

::

CursorArray
[2]

\rightarrow

Int

sumTree

arr

=

let

dcur

=

addrOfCursor

(
arr
[0])

in

let

lcur

=

addrOfCursor

(
arr
[1])

in

let

tagaddr

=

derefMutCursor

dcur

case

*
tagaddr

of

Leaf

\rightarrow

let

naddr

=

derefMutCursor

lcur

n

=

readInt
(
naddr
)

_

=

bumpMutableCursor

dcur

1

_

=

bumpMutableCursor

lcur

sizeof
(
Int
)

in

n

Node

\rightarrow

let

_

=

bumpMutableCursor

dcur

1

in

let

a

=

sumTree

arr

in

let

b

=

sumTree

arr

in

a

+

b

Figure 11
.
sumTree
in NoCal with mutable cursors

The code in Figure
11
shows
sumTree
in NoCal and gives an example of various cursor-related operations in NoCal.
The
Tree
datatype with the factored layout uses two buffers: one for data-constructor tags and one for
Leaf
payloads.
Therefore, the lowered function consumes
CursorArray
[2]
.
The function binds mutable references to both cursors at entry and bumps them in place as it traverses the tree.
Recursive calls receive the same cursor array, so cursor state is threaded through the traversal by in-place updates rather than by allocating fresh cursor bindings at each step.

Appendix D
Store Well-Formedness

The type-safety sketch in Section
3
relies on a runtime
store well-formedness judgement relating the static typing environments to the
runtime location map and store:

\mathit{\Sigma};\mathit{C};\mathit{A};\mathit{N}\vdash_{\mathit{wf}}\mathit{M};\mathit{S}

As in LoCal, this judgement bridges typed roots at the source level and
their concrete realizations at runtime. The key extension in SoCal is
that a typed root may be either a single symbolic location
{\mathit{l}}^{\mathit{r}}

or a factored root carrying component locations. In the latter case, store
well-formedness must guarantee not only that the tag component is present in
the store, but also that every projected field component and recursively nested
component location reachable from the root is itself well typed with respect to
the store.

D.1.
Top-level store well-formedness

Judgement form

\mathit{\Sigma};\mathit{C};\mathit{A};\mathit{N}\vdash_{\mathit{wf}}\mathit{M};\mathit{S}

The top-level store well-formedness judgement packages four classes of invariants.
First, every typed root in
\mathit{\Sigma}
must have a corresponding runtime
realization in
\mathit{M}
and
\mathit{S}
. Second, every symbolic location constraint
in
\mathit{C}
must be realized by the runtime configuration. Third, the allocation
state tracked by
\mathit{A}
and
\mathit{N}
must prevent overwriting and ensure that
allocation still points to the ends of the relevant buffers. Finally, locations
already typed in
\mathit{\Sigma}
must be disjoint from the nursery.

Definition

1.

If
({\mathit{l}}^{\mathit{r}}\mapsto\mathit{\tau})\in\mathit{\Sigma}
, then there exist

\mathit{i}_{s},\mathit{i}_{e}
such that

({\mathit{l}}^{\mathit{r}}\mapsto\langle\mathit{r},\mathit{i}_{s}\rangle)\in\mathit{M}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathit{\tau};\langle\mathit{r},\mathit{i}_{s}\rangle;\mathit{S}\vdash_{\mathit{ew}}\,\langle\mathit{r},\mathit{i}_{e}\rangle.

2.

If
(loc_{1}\mapsto\mathit{\tau})\in\mathit{\Sigma}
and

loc_{1}={l_{d}}^{r_{d}}\,\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},loc_{\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},loc_{\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},loc_{\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},loc_{\mathit{j}})}}
, then
there exist corresponding concrete components

\langle r_{d},\mathit{i}_{s}\rangle^{l_{d}}\,\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}

and

\langle r_{d},\mathit{i}_{e}\rangle^{l_{d}}\,\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}

such that

(loc_{1}\mapsto\langle r_{d},\mathit{i}_{s}\rangle^{l_{d}}\,\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}})\in\mathit{M}

and

\mathit{\tau};\langle r_{d},\mathit{i}_{s}\rangle^{l_{d}}\,\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}};\mathit{S}\vdash_{\mathit{ew}_{\mathit{rec}}}\langle r_{d},\mathit{i}_{e}\rangle^{l_{d}}\,\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}.

3.

The constraint environment is realized by the runtime configuration:

\mathit{C}\vdash_{wf_{cfc}}\mathit{M};\mathit{S}.

4.

The allocation and nursery environments are realized by the runtime
configuration:

\mathit{A};\mathit{N}\vdash_{wf_{ca}}\mathit{M};\mathit{S}.

5.

Typed roots and nursery locations are disjoint:

dom(\mathit{\Sigma})\cap\mathit{N}=\emptyset.

D.2.
End-witness judgements

Judgement forms

\mathit{\tau};\langle\mathit{r},\mathit{i}_{s}\rangle;\mathit{S}\vdash_{\mathit{ew}}\,\langle\mathit{r},\mathit{i}_{e}\rangle

\mathit{\tau};\langle r_{d},\mathit{i}_{s}\rangle^{l_{d}}\,\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})}};\mathit{S}\vdash_{\mathit{ew}_{\mathit{rec}}}\langle r_{d},\mathit{i}_{e}\rangle^{l_{d}}\,\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}{\overarrow@\to{(\mathit{K},\mathit{j},cloc_{e\mathit{j}})}}

The judgement
;;\vdash_{\mathit{ew}}\,
is the contiguous witness relation inherited
from LoCal: it states that the concrete location on the right is one past
the final cell of the value of type
\mathit{\tau}
rooted at the concrete location on
the left. The judgement
;;\vdash_{\mathit{ew}_{\mathit{rec}}}
extends this idea to factored
roots. It states that a factored concrete root has enough well-typed component
locations to traverse the entire materialized value, including recursive
descendants recovered through its component structure.

Contiguous witness relation

The contiguous witness relation follows the same structure as in LoCal.
For primitive fields such as integers, the witness advances by one cell. For
constructor-rooted values, the witness checks the constructor tag at the start
location, then recursively traverses the fields in layout order.

1.

If
\mathit{\tau}=\mathsf{Int}
and
\mathit{S}(\mathit{r})(\mathit{i}_{s})=\mathit{n}
, then

\mathsf{Int};\langle\mathit{r},\mathit{i}_{s}\rangle;\mathit{S}\vdash_{\mathit{ew}}\,\langle\mathit{r},\mathit{i}_{s}+1\rangle.

2.

If
\mathit{S}(\mathit{r})(\mathit{i}_{s})=\mathit{K}^{\prime}
and

\mathsf{data}\;\mathit{\tau}=\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{K}_{1}\;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}_{1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{K}_{1}\;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}_{1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{K}_{1}\;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}_{1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{K}_{1}\;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}_{1}}}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\mathit{K}^{\prime}\;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}^{\prime}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{K}_{m}\;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}_{m}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{K}_{m}\;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}_{m}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{K}_{m}\;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}_{m}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{K}_{m}\;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}}}_{m}}},

then the tag at
\langle\mathit{r},\mathit{i}_{s}\rangle
is a valid start for a
value of type
\mathit{\tau}
.

3.

Let
w_{0}=\mathit{i}_{s}+1
. For constructor fields

\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{1}}},\ldots,\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{n}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{n}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{n}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{n}}}
, require

\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{1}}};\langle\mathit{r},w_{0}\rangle;\mathit{S}\vdash_{\mathit{ew}}\,\langle\mathit{r},w_{1}\rangle

and

\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{j+1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{j+1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{j+1}}}{\overarrow@\to{\mathit{\tau}^{\prime}_{j+1}}};\langle\mathit{r},w_{j}\rangle;\mathit{S}\vdash_{\mathit{ew}}\,\langle\mathit{r},w_{j+1}\rangle\qquad j\in\{\,1,\ldots,n-1\,\}.

4.

The overall end witness is the end of the final field, or the cell one past
the tag if the constructor has no fields.

Recursive witness relation for factored roots

The recursive witness relation captures the additional invariants needed for
factored roots in factored layouts.

1.

The data-constructor component of the factored root is present at the tag
buffer location:

\mathit{S}(r_{d})(\mathit{i}_{s})=\mathit{K}^{\prime}

for some constructor
\mathit{K}^{\prime}
of type
\mathit{\tau}
.

2.

For each non-self-recursive field entry

(\mathit{K}^{\prime},\mathit{j},cloc_{s\mathit{j}})
in the component structure, the projected concrete
field component is well typed for the corresponding field type. Scalar fields and
datatypes laid out in an flattened form use the contiguous witness relation,
while nested factored fields may in turn require the recursive witness relation.

3.

Self-recursive fields omitted from the component structure are recovered by the
recursive structure of the layout. In the preorder fragment used in the main
paper, these descendants are reached by following the tag component and the
sequence of factored
(\mathsf{after}\;\cdot)
constraints generated by
T-DataConstructor-FullyFactored.
For instance, the first self recursive field starts at the end witness of the
construtor location (K’).
The subsequent end witnesses of each self-recursive field are bound by an
after relation, and can be recovered by applying the recursive end witness rule.

4.

The resulting end witness is the componentwise end of the entire rooted
value. In particular, the tag component yields the next constructor position
in its buffer, and the field components provide the corresponding end
witnesses for the non-tag buffers.

D.3.
Well-formedness of constructor progress constraints

Judgement form

\mathit{C}\vdash_{wf_{cfc}}\mathit{M};\mathit{S}

This judgement explains how the symbolic location constraints generated by the
typing rules are realized by the runtime configuration.

1.

If
(loc\mapsto(\mathsf{start}\;\mathit{r}))\in\mathit{C}
, then

\mathit{M}(loc)=\mathsf{ZeroIdx}(loc)
.

2.

If
(loc\mapsto loc^{\prime}+1)\in\mathit{C}
and
\mathit{M}(loc^{\prime})=\langle\mathit{r},\mathit{i}\rangle
, then

\mathit{M}(loc)=\langle\mathit{r},\mathit{i}+1\rangle
.

3.

If
(loc\mapsto(\mathsf{after}\;\mathit{\tau}@loc^{\prime}))\in\mathit{C}
and

\mathit{M}(loc^{\prime})=cloc^{\prime}
, then there exists
cloc^{\prime\prime}
such that

\mathit{\tau};cloc^{\prime};\mathit{S}\vdash_{\mathit{ew}}\,cloc^{\prime\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathit{M}(loc)=cloc^{\prime\prime}.

4.

If
({l_{d}}^{r_{d}}\mapsto(\mathsf{projTagLoc}\;loc_{1}))\in\mathit{C}
and

\mathit{M}(loc_{1})
is a factored concrete root, then its tag component is the
concrete location bound to
{l_{d}}^{r_{d}}
.

5.

If
(loc_{i}\mapsto(\mathsf{projFieldLoc}\;(\mathit{K},\mathit{i})\;loc_{1}))\in\mathit{C}
and

\mathit{M}(loc_{1})
is a factored concrete root whose component structure contains

(\mathit{K},\mathit{i},cloc_{i})
, then
\mathit{M}(loc_{i})=cloc_{i}
.

6.

If
(loc\mapsto(\mathsf{introLocVec}\;{l_{d}}^{r_{d}}\;flds))\in\mathit{C}
,
then
\mathit{M}(loc)
is the factored concrete root obtained by combining the
concrete tag location for
{l_{d}}^{r_{d}}
with the concrete field
locations denoted by the entries of
flds
.

D.4.
Well-formedness of allocation

Judgement form

\mathit{A};\mathit{N}\vdash_{wf_{ca}}\mathit{M};\mathit{S}

This judgement ensures that allocation remains linear and that no store cell is
overwritten.

1.

If
loc\in\mathit{N}
, then
loc
is mapped by
\mathit{M}
, but the concrete
store cell(s) denoted by that location have not yet been written in

\mathit{S}
.

2.

If
(\mathit{r}\mapsto loc)\in\mathit{A}
and
loc\in\mathit{N}
, then the concrete
location denoted by
loc
lies strictly past the highest written cell in
region
\mathit{r}
. For a factored location with multiple buffers,
the concrete loc will have the highest index across all buffers.

3.

If
(\mathit{r}\mapsto loc)\in\mathit{A}
and
loc\not\in\mathit{N}
, then the end
witness of the value rooted at
loc
lies at or past the current allocation
frontier for region
\mathit{r}
.

4.

If
(\mathit{r}\mapsto\emptyset)\in\mathit{A}
, then region
\mathit{r}
has no written
cells in
\mathit{S}
.
